在我的办公室里，父母最常讨论对孩子焦虑的担忧以及他们希望孩子如何“放手，享受生活，成为自己”。如果他们真诚，许多父母都会承认愤怒，沮丧，困惑和尴尬，特别是关于他们孩子在公共场合的行为。当孩子无法回答那些赞美他们的礼貌陌生人时，他们不知道如何回应。他们从善意的家庭成员那里得到（通常是不需要的）关于养育技能的建议，以及对孩子“强硬”的需要，从而迫使他们说话。最重要的是，父母通常不知道如何帮助他们的孩子练习勇敢。
有趣的是，对于有SM的孩子说，礼貌用语通常是最难的词，这增加了父母的尴尬。嗨，再见，对不起，拜托，谢谢，请原谅我是非常困难的，并促使孩子说这些话几乎总能导致立即避免。这可能是因为避免使用礼貌用语的广泛做法 - 所有这些都是父母提示孩子定期说话的话，因此这些孩子已经获得了许多避免这些提示的练习。此外，有选择性缄默症的儿童可能担心与他人一起使用这些词语会打开谈话或增加互动的大门。我经常鼓励父母在开始治疗时不要让孩子再说这些话。我们可以在以后将它们作为焦点或目标。
兄弟姐妹也受到SM的影响。有时，不止一个兄弟姐妹可能表现出SM的特征（特别是同卵双胞胎）。这是有道理的，因为我们知道焦虑具有遗传倾向，而一个兄弟可能会为其他人建模避免。或者，兄弟姐妹可能会开始为选择性缄默症的孩子说话，回答问题并培养一种特别外向和自信的性格来弥补他们姐妹或兄弟的安静。他们甚至可以介入并从有提示说话的场景中“拯救”孩子。
同龄人通常在社交上接受年龄较小的SM儿童，并且即使患有SM的孩子没有口头回应，他们也会继续玩耍和互动。然而，随着儿童年龄的增长，SM的特征（即，难以应对和开始口头和非语言，可能的社交焦虑，无法在小组项目中发挥作用等）开始疏远SM患儿与同龄人。随着友谊从互动游戏发展到通过沟通和共同理解培养的关系，他们的友谊数量可能会开始减少。随着年龄的增长，同龄人越来越不了解SM的个体并开始将他们排除在活动之外。如果不及时治疗，后来SM患儿最终成为社会弃儿的可能性更高 - 课堂上没有人真正记得，社交或邀请参加活动的孩子。
教师和学校工作人员经常会有与父母相似的反应 - 他们在关注孩子正在经历的事情，害怕通过焦虑的经历“打破”或“破坏”他们，以及沮丧，愤怒和困惑之间摇摆不定至于如何提供帮助。当教师负责评估孩子的知识并让孩子参与课堂活动时，选择性缄默可能会特别令教师感到困惑。此外，当孩子不与他们交谈时，许多老师承认自然受伤。当有SM的孩子无法与您交谈时，很容易接受它，但它可能与您和/或您的个性完全无关。关于SM的孩子与谁交谈，在什么环境以及在什么情况下，似乎都有不言而喻的“规则”。很多时候，孩子们自己无法解释或识别这些“规则”，但他们在孩子的行为中很明显。他们可以和足球的朋友聊天，但不能在学校聊天。他们可以在走到学校场地的同时与妈妈和爸爸交谈，但在踩到人行道上之后他们会口头上回应。有些孩子会和某些老师交谈但不会和其他老师交谈这些“规则”和界限似乎很少有韵律或理由，对同龄人和老师来说都是令人沮丧的。当有SM的孩子在搬到不同的环境时突然对他们说话时，朋友可能会受伤。当孩子不能跟她说话时，学校里最善良的老师可能会感到沮丧，但会和那个脾气暴躁，脾气暴躁的看门人说话。我们必须明白，有些因素我们并不知道，而且孩子并不知道这些因素会影响他们的谈话能力。这不是孩子或潜在的沟通伙伴的错，而只是孩子对我们不完全理解的焦虑的表现。

IMPLICATIONS OF THE AVOIDANCE PATTERN
The continued avoidance of speech in public scenarios has long-lasting academic, social, and behavioral outcomes. Children with SM tend to be well-behaved and are generally not disruptive even when unable to participate in class. However, teachers in today’s schools are trained to engage students in activities such as team-learning projects, oral presentations, partner reading, and group discussion. Classes promote not only listening but also speaking, reading, and participation, the bases of language comprehension and production. Thus, there are numerous long-lasting academic implications for kids with SM who are unable to participate in these educational skill-building activities. First, they are difficult to assess or test, particularly in reading. Because of the lack of speech, teachers and other school personnel may be unable to evaluate the child’s understanding of a concept, resulting in decreased opportunities to give corrective feedback. Second, the children may be unable to participate in group projects or activities. Many problem-solving techniques, including learning to compromise and work effectively with others, are learned within the context of group projects. Third, the children do not have the opportunity to practice oral presentations, show-and-tell, or public speaking. Finally, the children do not have the ability to ask a question or clarify information if they do not understand. For example, consider how math builds consistently on past knowledge. If a child does not understand a specific mathematical skill and is unable to ask for help or request that the teacher explain further, that child will likely fall behind in later math concepts. As the child struggles to demonstrate the knowledge they are acquiring, a gap opens between abilities and achievements (Sharoni, 2012).
In addition to academic implications, there are many social implications for children with SM. There are not higher rates of bullying concerning these children, but there may be difficulty making and keeping friends. Children with selective mutism, as they age, simply begin to fade into the social background. Even in early elementary school, kids with SM are treated differently. They may be treated as if they are younger or less capable than they really are by peers (who have the wonderful intention of “helping” them but accidentally reduce expectations and their opportunities to be verbal). Some are popular, but classmates assume a protective role and, despite the peers’ good intentions, this protection accidentally preserves the problem. The child with SM frequently becomes known as “the kid that doesn’t talk.”
Finally, there are behavioral implications in public and in extracurricular activities. Children with SM may be restricted in their participation in extracurricular activities if these activities have verbal demands that can’t be met by the child. More frightening is the thought that children with selective mutism cannot cry out for help if they are in danger or alert an adult if they are hurt or in trouble. One of my patients fell into a ditch on the way home from school. She injured her leg and ankle and could not escape from the ditch because it was too high to climb out. Because she was unable to cry out for help, she lay there for several hours before a search team found her. If she had been able to speak, she would have been heard almost immediately as there were several people within shouting distance.
If left untreated, there are many possible negative outcomes as the SM progresses (Steinhausen, 2005). The child is at a much higher risk for later generalized anxiety disorder, specific phobias, and major depressive disorder. They have a higher incidence of refusing to attend school and poor school performance as they become older. In adulthood, they are more likely to self-medicate with alcohol and marijuana and more likely to choose careers that do not demand frequent socialization. Since selective mutism is considered a disorder of childhood, adults with these symptoms may be diagnosed with severe social anxiety or avoidant personality disorder. These serious implications demonstrate the need for early intervention. If we intervene early and effectively, we can avoid the negative impact on the child and those around him/her.
Unfortunately, attempting to obtain early intervention can be confusing and misinformation is prevalent. Some of the literature and websites on selective mutism recommend that adults do not try to encourage the child to talk at all—that when all pressure is removed, the child will spontaneously begin speaking. Research does not support this premise that a total lack of intervention leads to verbalizations. It also does not make sense in the framework of conceptualizing selective mutism as a type of phobia. If you avoid any interaction with the object or event causing you anxiety, do you suddenly become brave and no longer frightened? No.
Consider the following example. Joey is afraid of dogs; because of his fear, Joey’s family and friends organize his daily life to avoid dogs altogether. The family stops going to houses of friends who have dogs. They throw away all books and videos featuring dogs. They even stop going on walks because of the potential of seeing a dog. Will Joey suddenly stop being afraid of dogs and decide they are wonderful pets? Will he say to his parents, “that fear of dogs was silly? I love dogs now”? Of course not. In fact, his fear may become more ingrained because he has not had any opportunity to experience dogs as non-threatening. The idea of dogs as dangerous will remain fixed.
In order to address and reduce his fear of dogs, his friends and family would need to slowly and gently introduce him to dogs that are safe, during which time he could begin to develop a different conceptualization of dogs. Instead of all dogs being seen as threatening, Joey would begin to see that some dogs are safe for interaction. Approaching a child’s fear this way helps them gain practice in facing their fears at a reasonable pace. In much the same way, if we remove all exposure to communication and all intervention encouraging the child to speak, it is extremely unlikely that they will suddenly talk.
Using the “wait and see” approach with children who have selective mutism can be very detrimental because of the way selective mutism develops—through a continuous pattern of avoiding communication. An approach that is inactive and allows that pattern to continue occurring only strengthens the pattern of avoidance. Therefore, each day that goes by in which the child does not work to practice new “brave behaviors” and instead continues the practice of avoidance is not neutral. Allowing the pattern of avoidance to continue makes it increasingly difficult to break.
Imagine being known as “the kid who doesn’t talk.” How challenging would it be to just start talking? Others will likely overreact to your first words, an outcome which kids with SM dread. “You talk. Say it again, say it again.” Intervening early saves children from being defined by this persona —“the child who doesn’t talk”—and reduces the response of others when they eventually communicate. Early intervention also assists in developing self-efficacy—the feeling that they are capable of being brave, making changes in their behavior, and reaching goals. Most of the children with SM describe a desire to talk; if we help them do so, they experience clear feelings of success. In a recent session, a first grader with SM and I were chatting, and in passing I asked her why she thought it was so easy to talk to me in my office but hard to talk to people in school. “Because you make me talk.” she replied with a smile. Chuckling, I asked her if perhaps everyone at school should “make” her talk. “Yes, I wish they would.” she responded. She desperately wants to talk to others, but simply cannot get the words out.
We as parents, teachers, school professionals, and mental health professionals want children to be successful and happy. Children with untreated selective mutism are likely not reaching this potential. We MUST intervene early to provide the best future outcomes.

避免模​​式的含义
在公共场景中继续避免言论具有长期的学术，社会和行为结果。患有SM的儿童往往表现良好，即使无法参加课堂也不会造成破坏。但是，今天学校的教师接受培训，让学生参与团队学习项目，口头报告，合作伙伴阅读和小组讨论等活动。课程不仅促进听力，还促进口语，阅读和参与，语言理解和生产的基础。因此，对于无法参加这些教育技能培养活动的SM儿童，有许多长期的学术含义。首先，它们很难评估或测试，特别是在阅读方面。由于缺乏言语，教师和其他学校工作人员可能无法评估孩子对概念的理解，导致提供纠正反馈的机会减少。其次，孩子可能无法参加团体项目或活动。许多解决问题的技术，包括学习妥协和与他人有效合作，都是在小组项目的背景下学习的。第三，孩子们没有机会练习口头陈述，表演和讲述或公开演讲。最后，如果孩子不理解，他们就没有能力提问或澄清信息。例如，考虑数学如何在过去的知识上建立一致。如果孩子不了解特定的数学技能并且无法寻求帮助或要求老师进一步解释，那么该孩子可能会在后来的数学概念中落后。当孩子努力展示他们所获得的知识时，能力和成就之间会出现差距（Sharoni，2012）。
除学术影响外，SM患儿还有许多社会影响。关于这些孩子的欺凌率并不高，但是制造和保持朋友可能有困难。随着年龄的增长，选择性缄默症的儿童开始逐渐淡出社会背景。即使在小学初期，有SM的孩子也会受到不同的待遇。他们可能被视为比同龄人更年轻或更少能力（他们的意图是“帮助”他们但却意外地降低了期望和他们的口头机会）。有些很受欢迎，但是同学们扮演着保护角色，尽管有同伴的良好意愿，但这种保护意外地保留了这个问题。患有SM的孩子经常被称为“不会说话的孩子”。
最后，在公共场合和课外活动中存在行为影响。如果这些活动具有儿童无法满足的口头要求，那么SM患儿可能会受限于参加课外活动。更可怕的是，有选择性缄默症的孩子如果处于危险之中就不能大声呼救，或者如果他们受伤或遇到麻烦就会提醒成年人。我的一名病人在放学回家的路上掉进了沟里。她腿部和脚踝受伤，无法逃离沟渠，因为太高而无法爬出去。因为她无法呼救，所以她在搜索小组找到她之前躺了几个小时。如果她能够说话，她几乎会立即被听到，因为有几个人在呼喊距离内。
如果不及时治疗，随着SM的进展，有许多可能的负面结果（Steinhausen，2005）。儿童患后期广泛性焦虑症，特定恐惧症和重度抑郁症的风险要高得多。随着年龄的增长，他们拒绝上学和学校表现不佳的发生率更高。在成年期，他们更有可能使用酒精和大麻进行自我治疗，更有可能选择不需要频繁社交的职业。由于选择性缄默症被认为是儿童期疾病，具有这些症状的成年人可能被诊断患有严重的社交焦虑或回避性人格障碍。这些严重影响表明需要早期干预。如果我们及早有效地进行干预，我们就可以避免对孩子及其周围的人产生负面影响。
不幸的是，试图获得早期干预可能会令人困惑，并且错误信息很普遍。一些关于选择性缄默症的文献和网站建议成年人不要试图鼓励孩子说话 - 当所有压力消除后，孩子会自发地开始说话。研究不支持这一前提，即完全缺乏干预会导致言语化。在将选择性缄默症概念化为一种恐惧症的框架中，它也没有意义。如果你避免与导致焦虑的物体或事件的任何互动，你会突然变得勇敢而不再受到惊吓吗？没有。
请考虑以下示例。乔伊害怕狗;由于他的恐惧，乔伊的家人和朋友组织他的日常生活，以避免狗完全。这家人不再去找有狗的朋友的房子。他们扔掉所有带狗的书籍和视频。他们甚至因为看到狗的潜力而停止散步。 Joey会突然停止害怕狗并决定他们是很棒的宠物吗？他会对他的父母说：“对狗的恐惧是愚蠢的吗？我现在爱狗了吗？当然不是。事实上，他的恐惧可能会变得更加根深蒂固，因为他没有任何机会将狗视为非威胁性的。狗是危险的想法将保持固定。
为了解决和减少他对狗的恐惧，他的朋友和家人需要慢慢地，温和地将他介绍给安全的狗，在此期间他可以开始对狗进行不同的概念化。而不是所有的狗被视为威胁，乔伊会开始看到一些狗是安全的互动。以这种方式接近孩子的恐惧有助于他们以合理的速度练习面对恐惧。以同样的方式，如果我们消除所有接触沟通和鼓励孩子说话的所有干预，他们就不太可能突然说话。
对选择性缄默的孩子使用“观望”方法可能是非常有害的，因为选择性缄默的发展方式 - 通过避免交流的连续模式。一种不活跃的方法并允许该模式继续发生只会加强避免模式。因此，孩子不去练习新的“勇敢行为”而不是继续避免行为的每一天都不是中立的。允许避免模式继续使得它越来越难以打破。
想象一下被称为“不会说话的孩子。”开始说话有多难？其他人可能会对你的第一句话反应过度，这是孩子们对SM的恐惧。 “你说。再说一遍，再说一遍。“早期干预可以避免孩子被这个角色定义 - ”不会说话的孩子“ - 当他们最终交流时减少他人的反应。早期干预也有助于培养自我效能感 - 他们能够勇敢，改变行为，达到目标的感觉。大多数患有SM的孩子都有说话的欲望;如果我们帮助他们这样做，他们会有明确的成功感受。在最近的一次会议上，一位有SM和我的一年级学生正在聊天，顺便说一句，我问她为什么在我的办公室里跟我说话很容易，但很难和学校里的人交谈。 “因为你让我说话。”她笑着回答。笑着，我问她，也许学校里的每个人都应该“说”她的谈话。 “是的，我希望他们愿意。”她回应道。她拼命想与别人交谈，但根本无法说出来。
我们作为父母，教师，学校专业人士和心理健康专业人士希望孩子们获得成功和快乐。未经治疗的选择性缄默症的儿童可能无法达到这种潜力。我们必须尽早介入，以提供最佳的未来结果。

CHAPTER 3
ASSESSING FOR SELECTIVE MUTISM
It may appear that a diagnosis of selective mutism is easy to make: If a child talks at home and does not talk at school or in public, they probably have selective mutism. The process of substantiating a diagnosis, sifting through related issues, and determining the best course of action can be much more complex. Like any psychological diagnosis, consideration of selective mutism involves researching the child and the family. However, unlike other diagnoses that may not take into consideration environmental factors, the diagnosis of selective mutism necessitates a thorough investigation of the child’s school and public life. Assessing for selective mutism (and ruling out other disorders) is a four- step process:
1. Diagnostic interview with parents/teacher/caregivers 
2. Questionnaires 
3. Observation of the child in different environments and in the presence of different individuals 
4. Rule out related or co-occurring disorders or weaknesses

第3章
评估选择性缄默症
似乎很容易做出选择性缄默症的诊断：如果一个孩子在家里说话并且不在学校或公共场合说话，他们可能会有选择性的缄默症。 证实诊断，筛选相关问题以及确定最佳行动方案的过程可能要复杂得多。 像任何心理诊断一样，选择性缄默症的考虑涉及研究儿童和家庭。 然而，与其他可能未考虑环境因素的诊断不同，选择性缄默症的诊断需要对儿童的学校和公共生活进行彻底调查。 评估选择性缄默症（并排除其他疾病）的过程分为四个步骤：
1.与父母/教师/护理人员进行诊断性访谈
2.问卷调查
3.在不同环境和不同个体面前观察孩子
4.排除相关或共存的疾病或弱点

DIAGNOSTIC INTERVIEW
A diagnostic interview is the first and most important piece of evidence in making a case for selective mutism. First, the clinician must determine the exact circumstances of the current behavior (not speaking). Through interviews with parents, teachers, and caregivers, the following information, in detail, must be obtained:
Who does the child currently talk to? Who are they unable to talk to (e.g., who are the people regularly in their lives with whom they cannot yet verbally communicate)? Who are they most likely or most interested in communicating with and who are they least likely or interested in communicating with?
What incentivizes the child to communicate, or in what circumstances would they be most likely to talk? If they need something? If someone asks them a direct question? If they are interacting one-on- one with an adult? If approached by a peer? It is likely that there are particular activities in which the child is more likely to talk (e.g., while engaged in a fun play activity as opposed to answering a question in front of the class).
Where is the child able to speak? Only at home? In public to parents if no one is watching or listening? On the playground, but not in the classroom?
How does the child communicate? Gestures? Writing? Sounds? Whispering? Short responses?
The more detail obtained during the interview, the more specific the treatment plan will be. For example, it would be a waste of time and energy to focus treatment on something the child is already easily able to do, such as responding to the parent in a store. Relatedly, we may not expect the child to speak immediately to the teacher’s assistant if the child has never done so before. For an example of a diagnostic interview, see page 107. Please note that the diagnostic interview is best conducted as an interactive, in-person interview, and not given as a questionnaire for parents to complete independently. This will allow the interviewer to follow up in obtaining more detail about the child’s modes of communicating.
诊断访谈
诊断性访谈是提出选择性缄默症的第一个也是最重要的证据。首先，临床医生必须确定当前行为的确切情况（不说话）。通过与父母，教师和看护人的访谈，必须获得以下详细信息：
孩子目前与谁交谈？谁不能与他们交谈（例如，谁是他们生活中经常与他们无法口头沟通的人）？谁最有可能或最有兴趣与谁沟通，谁最不可能或有兴趣与谁沟通？
是什么激励孩子进行交流，或者他们最有可能在什么情况下交谈？如果他们需要什么？如果有人问他们一个直接的问题？如果他们与成年人进行一对一的互动？如果是同伴接近？可能存在儿童更有可能说话的特定活动（例如，在参与有趣的游戏活动时，而不是在课堂前回答问题）。
孩子在哪里可以说话？只在家里？如果没有人在观看或收听，在公开场合向父母公开？在操场上，但不是在教室里？
孩子如何沟通？手势？写作？听起来？耳语？简短回答？
面试期间获得的细节越多，治疗计划就越具体。例如，将治疗集中在孩子已经能够轻易做到的事情上，例如回应商店中的父母，将浪费时间和精力。相关地，如果孩子以前从未这样做过，我们可能不会期望孩子立即与老师的助手说话。有关诊断性访谈的示例，请参阅第107页。请注意，诊断性访谈最好是作为交互式的面对面访谈进行，而不是作为父母独立完成的调查问卷。这将使面试官能够跟进，以获得有关孩子沟通方式的更多细节。
QUESTIONNAIRES AND RATING SCALES
Following a complete diagnostic interview, questionnaires and rating scales can compare children to “typically developing peers” in the realm of selective mutism symptoms as well as related anxiety symptoms. There is currently only one published questionnaire specific to selective mutism—the Selective Mutism Questionnaire (Bergman, 2008). The Selective Mutism Questionnaire (SMQ) was developed to obtain information about the child’s communication across three main settings—home, school, and public. The SMQ is a 17-item parent report questionnaire normed for children ages 3-11. Parents are asked to rate their child’s frequency of speech in several different settings and with different possible conversational partners. Parents rate the frequency of each item using a 4-point scale (3 = always, 2 = often, 1= seldom, 0 = never for speaking situations); thus, overall lower scores indicate less speech. Scores are then compared to a “clinical” group of children with selective mutism, as well as “typically developing” children to determine if speech production is within normal limits or if it is significantly discriminate from “typically developing peers.” The SMQ demonstrates high reliability in consistently identifying children who have selective mutism, without detecting children who have other forms of anxiety disorders.
The SMQ can be used in two ways—quantitatively and qualitatively. Quantitatively, the SMQ can be used to provide scores, as noted above, as evidence of a possible selective mutism diagnosis. Alternatively, it also can be used qualitatively in conjunction with Dr. Elisa Shipon-Blum’s Selective Mutism-Stages of Communication Comfort Scale© (SM-SCCS) to describe the severity of the selective mutism and build goals for school. The SM-SCCS is a guide to determining the child’s current stage of communication. See page 113 for the four stages of social communication.
In order to use the SM-SCCS qualitatively, users can look for the general trend in scores within each subsection. For instance, if parents scored mostly 0’s for their child’s speech in social situations, that child could be said to be functioning at Stage 0 in public settings. If most scores were 1’s in the school setting, the child could be said to be functioning generally in Stage 1 in the school setting. Using the SMQ and SM-SCCS in this way not only allows professionals and parents to describe the child’s current speech, but also provides descriptive verbiage for goal setting in special education plans (e.g., “Child will move from Stage 1 to Stage 3 with teacher in a one-on-one setting,” “Child will move from Stage 0 to Stage 1 in the general classroom setting,” etc.). More information on special education evaluation and planning will be provided in Chapter 7.
In addition to the SMQ and SM-SCCS, other anxiety-specific or general mental health questionnaires can be given to the child, parents, and teachers. One of the restrictions on using additional measures for children with selective mutism is age norms; most children diagnosed with selective mutism are quite young (younger than 8 years old), but many overall anxiety questionnaires are normed for children ages 8 and up. Some appropriate questionnaires include:
• The Screen for Childhood Anxiety Related Emotional Disorders (SCARED). The SCARED
provides both a self-report and parent-report form measuring symptoms of childhood anxiety disorders, including separation anxiety, social anxiety, generalized anxiety disorder, obsessive- compulsive disorder, panic disorder, traumatic stress disorder, and specific phobias. It is normed for children ages 8 to18.
• The Social Phobia and Anxiety Inventory for Children (SPAI-C). The SPAI-C requires a third
grade reading level, and allows the child to self-report on a range of potentially anxiety-provoking social situations. It is normed for children ages 8 to14 (Beidel, 1996).
• The KID-SCID (childhood disorders version of the Structured Clinical Interview for DSM-IV) is a structured interview with questions related to mood, anxiety, and disruptive behavior disorders for children ages 7-17 (Matzner, 1997).
• Multidimensional Anxiety Scale for Children (MASC). The MASC is used to assess major anxiety symptoms including physical symptoms of anxiety, social anxiety, separation anxiety, and panic disorder. It is normed for children ages 8-19 years (Pearson Education).
• Behavioral Assessment System for Children (BASC). The BASC is a behavioral rating scale and self-report personality scale which measures internalizing and externalizing symptoms as well as adaptive skills. It includes a self-report, parent-report, and teacher-report, and is normed for ages 4- 18 (Reynolds, 2004). 

问卷和评分标尺
在完整的诊断访谈之后，问卷和评级量表可以将儿童与选择性缄默症状以及相关焦虑症状领域的“典型发育阶段”进行比较。目前只有一篇专门针对选择性缄默症的已发表的调查问卷 - 选择性突变问卷（Bergman，2008）。选择性突变问卷（SMQ）的开发是为了获得有关儿童在家庭，学校和公众三个主要环境中的交流的信息。 SMQ是一份17项父母报告调查问卷，适用于3-11岁的儿童。要求家长在几个不同的环境和不同的可能的对话伙伴中评价孩子的发言频率。家长使用4分制评分每个项目的频率（3 =总是，2 =经常，1 =很少，0 =从不用于说话情况）;因此，整体较低的分数表示较少的语音。然后将得分与具有选择性缄默症的“临床”儿童组以及“通常正在发育”的儿童进行比较，以确定语音产生是否在正常范围内，或者是否与“通常正在发育的同龄人”明显不同.SMQ表现出高持续识别有选择性缄默症的儿童的可靠性，而不会发现患有其他形式焦虑症的儿童。
SMQ可以以两种方式使用 - 定量和定性。定量地，如上所述，SMQ可用于提供分数作为可能的选择性缄默症诊断的证据。或者，它也可以与Elisa Shipon-Blum博士的选择性突变 - 通信舒适量表©（SM-SCCS）一起定性地用于描述选择性缄默症的严重性并为学校建立目标。 SM-SCCS是确定儿童当前沟通阶段的指南。有关社交沟通的四个阶段，请参见第113页。
为了定性地使用SM-SCCS，用户可以在每个子部分中查找分数的总趋势。例如，如果父母在社交场合的孩子讲话时得分大多为0，则可以说该孩子在公共场所的0阶段正常运作。如果在学校环境中大多数分数是1分，那么可以说孩子在学校环境中的第1阶段中正常运作。以这种方式使用SMQ和SM-SCCS不仅可以让专业人士和家长描述孩子当前的演讲，还可以为特殊教育计划中的目标设定提供描述性的措辞（例如，“孩子将从教师的第1阶段进入第3阶段在一对一的设置中，“”儿童将在一般教室环境中从第0阶段移至第1阶段，“等等。有关特殊教育评估和规划的更多信息将在第7章中提供。
除了SMQ和SM-SCCS之外，还可以向孩子，父母和教师提供其他焦虑或一般的心理健康问卷。对选择性缄默儿童使用额外措施的限制之一是年龄规范;大多数被诊断患有选择性缄默症的儿童都很年轻（小于8岁），但许多整体焦虑问卷都是针对8岁及以上儿童的标准。一些适当的问卷包括：
•儿童焦虑相关情绪障碍筛查（SCARED）。 SCARED
提供自我报告和父母报告形式，测量儿童焦虑症的症状，包括分离焦虑，社交焦虑，广泛性焦虑症，强迫症，恐慌症，创伤性应激障碍和特定恐惧症。它适用于8至18岁的儿童。
•社交恐怖症和儿童焦虑量表（SPAI-C）。 SPAI-C需要第三名
年级阅读水平，并允许孩子自我报告一系列可能引起焦虑的社交情况。它适用于8至14岁的儿童（Beidel，1996）。
•KID-SCID（DSM-IV结构化临床访谈的儿童紊乱版本）是针对7-17岁儿童的情绪，焦虑和破坏性行为障碍相关问题的结构化访谈（Matzner，1997）。
•儿童多维焦虑量表（MASC）。 MASC用于评估主要焦虑症状，包括焦虑，社交焦虑，分离焦虑和恐慌症的身体症状。它适用于8-19岁的儿童（Pearson Education）。
•儿童行为评估系统（BASC）。 BASC是一种行为评定量表和自我报告的人格量表，用于衡量内在化和外化症状以及适应性技能。它包括自我报告，家长报告和教师报告，并为4至18岁的人提供标准（Reynolds，2004）。

DIRECT STRUCTURED OBSERVATION
Prior to designing treatment, many questions need to be answered: what verbal communication needs to be shaped, which people and environments need to be introduced, which reinforcements are most helpful and most likely to be easily employed in the home and classroom, and what accidental reinforcement is being provided from teachers, peers, parents, siblings, and friends. While interviews and questionnaires can garner helpful data, direct observations can provide a wealth of information. In fact, behavioral observations have been described as the “key lynchpin” of assessment and treatment of selective mutism (Kearney, 2006). In order to help plan treatment, the clinician should engage the child in a structured observation. It is beneficial, if possible, to do an observation in three main settings:
1. At home
a. A videotape made by parents of the child when they are comfortable, relaxed, and verbal. 
b. An interaction with the clinician in the home setting (i.e., the clinician visits the child’s home to learn more about the family dynamic and play with the child in the environment where they likely feel the most comfortable). 
2. In the school 
a. The clinician sits quietly and discretely in the classroom, watching the group factors that may influence the child’s speech or lack of speech (see the information on functional behavioral analysis below). 
3. In a novel location (such as a psychologist’s office, a restaurant, or a store)—a sample structured observation sheet is included on page 114. 
a. The clinician observes the child one-on-one with the parent in a novel setting, such as an office playroom. 
b. The clinician observes the outcome of introducing a novel adult or child into the interaction. 
c. The novel person prompts the child with questions, increasing in difficulty level (from yes/no questions to forced choice questions and ending with open-ended questions). 
d. The novel person exits the room, and the clinician observes the child as they return to baseline.
If the child does not already know the clinician, the first observation should be in the classroom. This would allow the clinician to observe the child without awareness that he is the focus of the observation. As a psychologist, I perform the public observation in my office. In my office, there is a playroom with a one-way mirror and a sound system that allows me to watch the interactions between parents and children in a play situation. However, even if there is no access to a one-way mirror, an effective observation could be done with a webcam in the room. Finally, an observation could be made by an examiner sitting in the same room as unobtrusively as possible (with the expectation that their presence may change the behavior of the child; therefore, this is not a preferable way of conducting an observation if any other mode is possible).

直接结构化观察
在设计治疗之前，需要回答许多问题：需要形成什么样的口头交流，需要引入哪些人和环境，哪些增援最有帮助，最容易在家庭和教室中使用，以及什么是偶然的教师，同龄人，父母，兄弟姐妹和朋友正在加强。虽然访谈和问卷调查可以获得有用的数据，但直接观察可以提供丰富的信息。事实上，行为观察被描述为评估和治疗选择性缄默症的“关键关键”（Kearney，2006）。为了帮助计划治疗，临床医生应该让孩子参与结构化观察。如果可能的话，在三个主要环境中进行观察是有益的：
1.在家里
一个。由孩子的父母在舒适，放松和口头表达时制作的录像带。
湾在家庭环境中与临床医生的交互（即，临床医生访问孩子的家以更多地了解家庭动态并在他们可能感觉最舒适的环境中与孩子一起玩耍）。
在学校里
一个。临床医生静静地，独立地坐在教室里，观察可能影响孩子讲话或缺乏言语的群体因素（参见下面关于功能行为分析的信息）。
3.在一个新的位置（如心理学家的办公室，餐馆或商店） - 第114页包含样本结构化观察表。
一个。临床医生在新颖的环境中与父母一对一地观察孩子，例如办公室游戏室。
湾临床医生观察将新的成人或儿童引入相互作用的结果。
C。这个小说的人提示孩子有问题，难度水平增加（从是/否问题到强迫选择问题，以开放式问题结束）。
d。这个新人退出房间，临床医生在孩子回到基线时观察孩子。
如果孩子还不知道临床医生，第一次观察应该在教室里。这将允许临床医生观察孩子而不会意识到他是观察的焦点。作为心理学家，我在办公室进行公众观察。在我的办公室里，有一个带单向镜子和音响系统的游戏室，让我可以在游戏情况下观看父母和孩子之间的互动。但是，即使无法访问单向镜像，也可以使用房间内的网络摄像头进行有效观察。最后，可以由坐在同一房间内的审查员尽可能不引人注意地进行观察（期望他们的存在可能会改变孩子的行为;因此，如果有任何其他模式，这不是进行观察的优选方式是可能的）。

STRUCTURED SCHOOL OBSERVATION
The process of conducting a structured school observation can provide an enormous amount of data and information to the assessment of selective mutism (Schill, 1996). In order to provide information for treatment planning, an observation would take into consideration the same questions asked in the diagnostic intake: who does the child talk to, where does the child talk, and how does he or she communicate? The clinician can code communicative behaviors (e.g., gestures, whispering, mouthing, sounds, one-word responses, multiple-word responses, longer utterances, or initiating). Additionally, it is helpful to note to whom the child directs communication (e.g., known adult vs. novel adult, known peer vs. novel peer). The clinician should track where the child communicates (e.g., one-on-one, in a small group, in front of a larger group). Finally, it is important to record key antecedents (e.g., demands/expectations of the situation or lack thereof), outcomes or consequences of the mutism, and possible reinforcers used to produce speech. Generally, the function of the mutism is predicted to be avoidance-based. A sample school observation sheet is included on page 116.
Information garnered through the direct structured observation and classroom observation can be used to determine what intervention technique is recommended. In Chapter 4, these data will be considered in determining whether to use shaping or fading techniques in treatment.
It is important to note that the child with selective mutism may surprise you by speaking to you in the evaluation, particularly if the evaluation occurs in a one-on-one private setting. Kurtz (2011) reported that when evaluating students with SM following a brief rapport-building interaction, 27% of the children responded to the examiner’s first question, 36% responded to the second question, and 43% responded to the third question asked by the examiner. This should not necessarily rule out selective mutism, but should be noted for consideration when determining the best intervention.

固有模式学校的观察
进行结构化学校观察的过程可以为选择性缄默症的评估提供大量的数据和信息（Schill，1996）。为了提供治疗计划的信息，观察会考虑诊断摄入中提出的相同问题：孩子与谁交谈，孩子在哪里说话，以及他或她如何交流？临床医生可以编码交流行为（例如，手势，耳语，口腔，声音，单词响应，多词响应，更长的话语或发起）。另外，注意孩子指导沟通的人是有帮助的（例如，已知成人与新成人，已知同伴与新同伴）。临床医生应该跟踪孩子在哪里进行通信（例如，一对一，在一个小组中，在一个较大的组前面）。最后，重要的是记录关键的前因（例如，对情况的要求/期望或缺乏），缄默的结果或后果，以及用于产生言语的可能的强化物。通常，预测缄默症的功能是基于回避的。第116页包含示例学校观察表。
通过直接结构化观察和课堂观察获得的信息可用于确定推荐的干预技术。在第4章中，将在确定是否在治疗中使用整形或褪色技术时考虑这些数据。
值得注意的是，选择性缄默症的孩子可能会在评估中与您说话，这可能会让您感到惊讶，特别是如果评估是在一对一的私人环境中进行的。 Kurtz（2011）报告说，在通过简短的关系建立互动对SM学生进行评估时，27％的孩子回答了考官的第一个问题，36％回答了第二个问题，43％回答了第三个问题。考官。这不一定排除选择性缄默症，但在确定最佳干预时应注意考虑。

RULING OUT CO-OCCURRING DISORDERS
As discussed in the previous chapters, there are many different disorders that can occur in conjunction with selective mutism, including other anxiety disorders and speech/language weaknesses or disorders. There are also many diagnoses that may share symptoms with selective mutism, such as autism spectrum disorders. The main differential symptom between selective mutism and other anxiety disorders, developmental disorders, or language-based disorders is that the child with selective mutism can talk in certain situations, but is not able to use that same quality/consistency/volume of speech in other situations due to anxiety. Thus, a child could have an autism spectrum disorder and selective mutism, if they are able to speak in one situation (despite the quality or social appropriateness of that speech) and not in a more public environment. The characteristic differentiating autism from selective mutism is the clear gap between the child’s behaviors in a “safe” or comfortable environment, such as in the home setting, and behavior in social or performance settings, such as the school environment. In order to evaluate for these other disorders, a full psycho-educational evaluation could also include (but is not limited to):
• speech/language evaluation
• evaluation of intellectual functioning (a multi-dimensional nonverbal intelligence scale, such as the
Leiter-R may be most appropriate)
• educational evaluation
• hearing evaluation
• oral-motor examination of coordination and strength in the muscles of the jaw, tongue and lips
• evaluation for autism symptoms (such as the Autism Diagnostic Observation Schedule). Please note
that many of the scoring criteria of the ADOS rely on communication with the examiner, and the child with selective mutism may not be able to communicate consistently either verbally or nonverbally. Thus, the ADOS should only be used as one piece of data in an evaluation for autism.

排除并发性疾病
正如前面章节中所讨论的，有许多不同的疾病可以与选择性缄默症一起发生，包括其他焦虑症和言语/语言弱点或障碍。还有许多诊断可能与选择性缄默症共享症状，例如自闭症谱系障碍。选择性缄默症与其他焦虑症，发育障碍或基于语言的障碍之间的主要差别症状是，选择性缄默症的孩子在某些情况下可以说话，但在其他情况下不能使用相同的质量/一致性/音量。焦虑的情况。因此，如果孩子能够在一种情况下说话（尽管该语言的质量或社会适宜性）而不是在更公共的环境中，则儿童可能患有自闭症谱系障碍和选择性缄默症。自闭症与选择性缄默症的区别在于儿童在“安全”或舒适环境中的行为（例如在家庭环境中）与社交或表演环境中的行为（例如学校环境）之间的明显差距。为了评估这些其他疾病，完整的心理教育评估还可以包括（但不限于）：
•言语/语言评估
•评估智力功能（多维非语言智力量表，如Leiter-R可能是最合适的）
•教育评估
•听力评估
•口腔 - 运动检查下颌，舌头和嘴唇肌肉的协调性和力量
•评估自闭症症状（如自闭症诊断观察时间表）。请注意
ADOS的许多评分标准依赖于与检查者的沟通，而选择性缄默症的孩子可能无法通过口头或非口头方式进行一致的沟通。因此，ADOS应仅用作自闭症评估中的一个数据。
MAKING A DIAGNOSIS
Who can make a diagnosis? For an official diagnosis, the determination must be made by a clinical psychologist, licensed clinical mental health counselor, clinical social worker, psychiatric nurse, speech/language pathologist, or physician (such as a pediatrician or psychiatrist). However, many other individuals are also very important in both the evaluation and treatment team. One of the most important and helpful professionals is the speech/language pathologist.
Speech/language pathologists (SLP) can receive a lot of contradictory messages on their role in the evaluation and treatment of selective mutism. At times, they are encouraged to be involved, but in other situations they may be told that selective mutism is an anxiety disorder and should therefore be addressed by professionals who have expertise in anxiety. They themselves may be uncomfortable with the idea of being involved in the treatment of an anxiety disorder. Is selective mutism an anxiety disorder? Yes. Is selective mutism a pragmatic communication disorder? Yes. Social interactions are the basis for language learning, and children with reduced social interactions may have fewer opportunities for language learning, feedback, and practice. Therefore, having an SLP on the evaluation and treatment team can be extremely helpful.
The role of the SLP in the evaluation team is obvious—ruling out fundamental communication disorders and describing symptoms, situations, and weaknesses that may impact the child’s ability to use speech consistently and correctly. As noted before, if the child has an underlying communication problem, he or she is at a higher risk for selective mutism and can have a longer treatment timeline. The American Speech Language Hearing Association lists multiple roles and responsibilities for the SLP, including assessing and treating the social aspects of communication, or social pragmatics. These can and should be included in the evaluation of a child with selective mutism, and should be considered when making a diagnosis.
A speech and language assessment of a child with selective mutism might include:
• Speech production
• Fluency (the rhythm of speech)
• Receptive language (what the child understands)
• Expressive language (what the child can convey verbally, including word-finding, sentence
formulation, narrative quality, etc.)
• Pragmatic language (knowledge of the social rules of speech)
• Articulation (how speech sounds are made)
With this information, an SLP can determine if the child’s speech and language skills limit him/her socially, if they are using language appropriately, and if there are sufficient opportunities to communicate. Possible treatment targets may include motor planning and articulation, syntax and morphology, increasing sentence length and complexity, addressing word-finding weaknesses, sentence formation, and teaching/rehearsing pragmatics of speech.
Children with selective mutism can be quite a challenge to evaluate in a reliable, valid manner. Their difficulty responding both verbally and nonverbally, slowness to response or inefficiency working quickly, and avoidance of interacting with novel adults can easily lead to much lower scores on tests and, without consideration of the impact of anxiety, a misinterpretation of the child’s ability level. Additionally, anxiety can mask a child’s language abilities (Klein, 2012). We as practitioners must be very careful in the interpretation of our test results, as it is unfair to a child if we take the scores we receive as their overall level of potential.
Recently at a presentation for SLPs, I was discussing the tendency for scores to be an under- estimate of the child’s ability, both cognitively and academically, when one of the participants raised her hand to share an experience. She had been working with a child diagnosed with selective mutism who had been referred to the school psychologist for a cognitive evaluation. When the evaluation team met to review the testing results, the psychologist provided his diagnosis of cognitive impairment, based on extremely low scores in intelligence testing. This was not the child the SLP knew—the child appeared quite intelligent, and his teachers reported that he always did well on written work. When asked to provide more information on the testing, the psychologist admitted that the child “chose not to respond” and therefore received a zero score on most items, resulting in an IQ score in the severely impaired range. This circumstance resulted in an invalid interpretation of scores. Thus, it is important to be cognizant of the effect of selective mutism on all testing, and to be sensitive to the resulting outcomes.
Professionals (both speech/language pathologists and psychologists) can increase the validity of evaluation outcomes for children with selective mutism by using a few “alternative” assessment strategies:
• First, the assessor should attempt to develop a relationship with the child, as opposed to jumping directly into testing. Prior to the evaluation, the evaluator may consider having 2-3 sessions with the child in a fun, play-based interaction without the expectation for speech.
• The evaluation should be completed either one-on-one with the evaluator or with only the child, parent, and evaluator in the room. Some children are able to speak to or read to their parent with another individual in the room listening.
• The assessor should take care not to engage the child in unnecessary eye-contact, as sustained eye- contact from unfamiliar people can make children with selective mutism uncomfortable (Shipon- Blum, 2003). That does not mean no eye-contact, but that the examiner should “busy herself” when possible with other activities and limit direct eye gaze.
• Parents may be used as test presenters. In fact, research has shown that parents trained in test presentations can be effective evaluators and can produce significantly higher language test scores in comparison to professionals (Klein, 2012).
• Evaluators may have to use more non-specific and qualitative evaluations of speech through videotapes or audiotapes of the children in comfortable situations.
When the evaluator has data suggesting a pattern of speaking comfortably in at least one environment versus a lack of speech in more public environments, and other reasons for this difficulty in speaking have been ruled out through a diagnostic interview, clinical observation, psycho-educational evaluation (including speech, hearing, and differential diagnosis consideration), we can feel confident in diagnosing selective mutism. And luckily, this evaluation plan provides a wealth of great information to prepare for the next step—treatment.

做出诊断
谁可以做出诊断？对于官方诊断，必须由临床心理学家，持牌临床心理健康顾问，临床社会工作者，精神科护士，言语/语言病理学家或医生（如儿科医生或精神科医生）进行确定。然而，许多其他人在评估和治疗团队中也非常重要。语言/语言病理学家是最重要和最有帮助的专业人士之一。
言语/语言病理学家（SLP）可以收到很多关于他们在选择性缄默症的评估和治疗中的作用的矛盾信息。有时鼓励他们参与其中，但在其他情况下，他们可能会被告知选择性缄默症是一种焦虑症，因此应该由具有焦虑专业知识的专业人士来解决。他们自己可能对参与治疗焦虑症的想法感到不安。选择性缄默症是一种焦虑症吗？是。选择性缄默症是一种务实的沟通障碍吗？是。社交互动是语言学习的基础，社交互动减少的儿童可能缺乏语言学习，反馈和实践的机会。因此，在评估和治疗团队中获得SLP可能非常有帮助。
SLP在评估团队中的作用是显而易见的 - 排除基本的沟通障碍，并描述可能影响孩子一致和正确使用语言能力的症状，情况和弱点。如前所述，如果孩子存在潜在的沟通问题，他或她处于选择性缄默症的风险较高，并且可以有更长的治疗时间表。美国语言听力协会列出了SLP的多重角色和职责，包括评估和处理沟通的社会方面或社会语用学。这些可以而且应该包含在对有选择性缄默症的儿童的评估中，并且在做出诊断时应该考虑。
对有选择性缄默的儿童进行语言和语言评估可能包括：
•演讲制作
•流利（语音节奏）
•接受语言（孩子理解的内容）
•表达语言（孩子可以口头传达的内容，包括找词，句子
制定，叙事质量等）
•实用语言（社会言语规则的知识）
•清晰度（如何发出语音）
有了这些信息，SLP可以确定孩子的言语和语言技能是否会限制他/她的社交，是否正确使用语言，以及是否有足够的机会进行交流。可能的治疗目标可能包括运动计划和清晰度，语法和形态，增加句子长度和复杂性，解决词语发现缺陷，句子形成，以及教授/排练语言语用学。
选择性缄默症的儿童以可靠，有效的方式进行评估可能是一项挑战。他们在口头和非口语上的反应困难，反应缓慢或效率低下，以及避免与新成人互动，很容易导致测试得分低得多，并且在不考虑焦虑的影响的情况下，误解了孩子的能力水平。此外，焦虑可以掩盖孩子的语言能力（Klein，2012）。我们作为从业者在解释我们的测试结果时必须非常小心，因为如果我们将我们收到的分数作为他们的整体潜力水平，这对孩子是不公平的。
最近在一篇关于SLP的演讲中，我正在讨论当一位参与者举手分享经验时，分数是否会在认知和学术上低估孩子的能力。她一直在与一名被诊断患有选择性缄默症的孩子一起工作，并被提交给学校心理学家进行认知评估。当评估小组开会审查测试结果时，心理学家根据智力测试中的极低分数提供了他的认知障碍诊断。这不是SLP知道的孩子 - 这个孩子看起来非常聪明，他的老师报告说他总是在写作上做得很好。当被要求提供有关测试的更多信息时，心理学家承认孩子“选择不回应”，因此在大多数项目上获得零分，导致智力分数在严重受损范围内。这种情况导致对分数的无效解释。因此，重要的是要认识到选择性缄默对所有测试的影响，并对所得到的结果敏感。
专业人士（语言/语言病理学家和心理学家）可以通过使用一些“替代”评估策略来提高选择性缄默症儿童评估结果的有效性：
•首先，评估员应该尝试与孩子建立关系，而不是跳跃直接进入测试。在评估之前，评估者可以考虑在有趣的，基于游戏的交互中与孩子进行2-3次会话而不期望发言。
•评估应与评估员一对一完成，或者只与房间中的孩子，家长和评估员一起完成。有些孩子可以和房间里的另一个人一起听父母讲话或读书。
•评估员应注意不要让孩子接触不必要的眼神，因为不熟悉的人的持续眼神接触会使选择性缄默症的孩子感到不舒服（Shipon-Blum，2003）。这并不意味着没有目光接触，但审查员应该尽可能“忙碌”其他活动，并限制直接注视。
•父母可以用作测试演示者。事实上，研究表明，接受过测试演示培训的家长可以成为有效的评估者，与专业人士相比可以产生更高的语言测试成绩（Klein，2012）。
•评估者可能必须通过孩子们在舒适的情况下的录像带或录音带对其语言进行更多非特定和定性的评估。
当评估者的数据表明至少在一个环境中说话的模式与在更多公共环境中缺乏言语时相比，并且通过诊断访谈，临床观察，心理教育评估排除了说话困难的其他原因。 （包括言语，听力和鉴别诊断考虑），我们可以对诊断选择性缄默症有信心。幸运的是，这个评估计划提供了大量的信息，为下一步治疗做好准备。

CHAPTER 4
RE-WRITING THE AVOIDANCE SCRIPT
Following the evaluation and diagnosis of selective mutism, we begin the fun (but admittedly challenging) step—the intervention. The main sections of intervention discussed in this chapter include:
1. Determining the intervention “team” and main interventionists 2. Building rapport between the child and interventionists 3. Educating the child about his/her anxiety 4. Developing the behavioral treatment plan, including a communication ladder (shaping) and stimulus fading plan。
This chapter is arguably the most practical, as it provides the “meat” of the intervention. Please consider making notes in the margins and filling in the workbook sections as you proceed.

第四章
重写逃避免脚本
在选择性缄默症的评估和诊断之后，我们开始了有趣（但无可否认具有挑战性）的步骤 - 干预。 本章讨论的主要干预部分包括：
1.确定干预“团队”和主要干预者
2.在儿童和干预者之间建立融洽关系
3.教育孩子关于他/她的焦虑
4.制定行为治疗计划，包括沟通阶梯（塑形）和刺激衰退计划。
本章可以说是最实用的，因为它提供了干预的“肉”。 在您继续操作时，请考虑在边距中做笔记并填写工作簿部分。

DETERMINING THE INTERVENTION “TEAM” AND MAIN INTERVENTIONISTS
In Chapter 2, the conceptualization of selective mutism identified avoidance as a coping strategy. The child becomes overwhelmed with anxiety and tries to avoid the interaction or occurrence which is causing the anxiety. An important piece of the intervention involves what often happens next— individuals accidentally step in and rescue the child. This behavior is born of the desire to comfort the child and reduce the anxiety, but can backfire when adults, peers, parents, teachers, and siblings help the child maintain the avoidance behavior. There are many ways that “rescuing” occurs:
• Parents respond for the child (e.g., order for the child at restaurants, answer for the child, tell extended family members what the child wants, etc.)
• Teachers stop asking the child questions in class
• Peers stop interacting verbally with the child in class
• Peers or siblings quickly state, “Jamie does not talk.” or answer for the child when others ask a question
• Others provide the child with easy nonverbal communication tools (e.g., only ask yes/no questions to allow head shaking/nodding, accept and encourage written responses, etc.)
To begin building the intervention team, it is important to begin thinking about those individuals in the child’s life who accidentally rescue and reinforce the avoidance. Who may be “accidental reinforcers” in the child’s life, and what do they do to help maintain the avoidance of speech?

确定干预“团队”和主要干预措施
在第2章中，选择性缄默症的概念化将避免视为应对策略。孩子变得焦虑不安，并试图避免引起焦虑的相互作用或发生。干预的一个重要部分涉及接下来经常发生的事情 - 个人意外地介入并拯救孩子。这种行为源于安慰孩子和减少焦虑的愿望，但当成年人，同龄人，父母，老师和兄弟姐妹帮助孩子维持避免行为时，可能会适得其反。有许多方法可以“拯救”：
•父母为孩子做出回应（例如，在餐馆为孩子订购，为孩子回答，告诉大家庭成员孩子想要什么，等等）
•教师不再在课堂上询问孩子的问题
•同伴停止在课堂上与孩子口头互动
•同伴或兄弟姐妹很快说出“杰米不会说话。”或在别人提问时为孩子回答
•其他人为孩子提供简单的非语言交流工具（例如，只询问是/否问题以允许头部摇晃/点头，接受并鼓励书面回复等）
要开始建立干预团队，重要的是要开始考虑孩子生命中那些意外拯救和加强避免的人。谁可能是孩子生命中的“意外强化者”，他们如何帮助维持言语的避免？

Person How do they inadvertently reinforce avoidance?
人们如何无意中加强了逃避行为？
These accidental reinforcers are those individuals that we particularly need on the treatment “team”—we need them to become knowledgeable about selective mutism and learn ways to stop reinforcing the avoidance. A “team” in the general sense of the word (which broadly encapsulates those who know the child’s strengths/weaknesses and are armed with some strategies to assist in building “brave” skills) may include:
• Parents
• Teachers
• School administrators
• Speech/language pathologists
• Social workers
• School psychologists
• School counselors
• Behavioral analyst/behavioral specialist
• Clinical psychologists
• Classroom peers/friends
• Siblings
• Extended family, particularly common caregivers
However, a core treatment team (those responsible for carrying out the actual intervention) includes the parents, the keyworker within the school setting, and the mental health professional outside of the school setting (e.g., behavioral analyst, clinical psychologist). All members have different responsibilities:
A keyworker is the individual inside the school setting who is responsible for:
1. Managing and implementing the behavioral intervention inside the school setting 
2. Generalizing speech to all school environments and with as many people as possible 
3. Communicating with the teacher, parents, and treating psychologist
Most frequently, a keyworker is a person with either a mental health or speech/language background and training, such as a school psychologist, behavioral analyst, social worker, counselor, or speech/language pathologist. However, determining a keyworker who can regularly meet with the child is difficult—professionals are overworked, caseloads are at capacity, and schools may have a limited number of special education personnel. Therefore, the functional or realistic criteria for a keyworker are someone who can consistently work with the child, is caring and interested in the intervention, is willing to be flexible and learn more about selective mutism, and is a good personality match for the child. I have been on treatment teams where the keyworker was a principal, school nurse, teaching assistant, paraprofessional, and reading specialist, among others. The teacher is not recommended as a keyworker. This is not because of training, ability level, or desire, but simply because teachers usually have 30+ children regularly in their care and finding time to focus one-on-one regularly with a specific child may put too much stress on an already stressful job. It is usually preferable that another individual take the position of the keyworker but communicate very closely with the teacher.
Another important characteristic of a keyworker is availability for consistent intervention. Today’s schools encourage group pull-outs for special education services. This likely will not work for the child with selective mutism—at least not at first. Although there are some notable exceptions (such as children who speak more easily when they are in the room with a friend or sibling), in order for most children to be successful, they need to first meet with the keyworker in one-on-one interactions on a frequent basis. In order to make consistent and significant progress, meeting individually with the child for 15 minutes three times per week is the minimum necessary. There is a good reason for this, these children are practicing using avoidance strategies every single day, many times per day. In order to change their behavioral repertoire, we need to practice brave behaviors many times per day. The more frequent the practices, the better. If practices occur fewer than three times per week, it is very hard to get traction and make progress. Meeting once per week or once every other week, as is the norm for most special education services in the school, is simply not frequent enough to encourage change.
Some schools have dealt with this issue by naming two keyworkers. For instance, the social worker may meet with the child twice per week and the SLP may meet with him twice per week. It is great to have multiple communication partners within the school, and generally this works well if both keyworkers regularly communicate with one another. It should be expected that the child may progress slightly more slowly at first, as they must become desensitized to two different people instead of one.
The mental health professional outside of the school is responsible for:
1. Developing and maintaining the treatment plan through consultation with the school. 
2. Training parents on strategies to increase communication with extended family/friends and in public settings. 
3. Carrying out “brave practices” in public settings, such as ordering at restaurants, calling people on the phone, asking where to find items in the store, and approaching peers on the playground.
When seeking out a psychologist, behavior analyst or mental health professional to assist in the treatment, it is important to search for some key characteristics:
• Experience working with children, particularly children with anxiety disorders.
• Training and experience in behavioral therapy or cognitive behavioral therapy, and use of these as the main treatment modality.
• A willingness to learn about selective mutism (as it is treated slightly differently from other childhood anxiety disorders).
• A good rapport with the parent, child, and school, and a willingness to work productively with all three.
If you have a mental health professional with expertise or extensive experience in treating selective mutism in your area. . . you are lucky. These individuals can be found on the Selective Mutism Group website at www.selectivemutism.org (search under “Find Help”). Most areas do not have experts in their backyard, and therefore parents must find someone with the qualifications above through good, old- fashioned detective work (otherwise known as calling the psychologist’s office and asking the right questions). If it is at all financially possible, parents are encouraged to make the decision regarding a treating professional on expertise, ability, and fit—not whether they accept the child’s health insurance. While it is understandable that financial considerations are important and necessary for families, effective early intervention is so imperative that wasting time in an ineffective treatment might not be worth the money saved.

这些意外强化者是我们在治疗“团队”中特别需要的那些人 - 我们需要他们对选择性缄默症有所了解并学习如何停止强化避免。一般意义上的“团队”（广泛地包含那些了解孩子的优点/缺点并且拥有一些策略来帮助建立“勇敢”技能的人）可能包括：
•父母
• 教师
•学校管理员
•言语/语言病理学家
• 社会工作者
•学校心理学家
•学校辅导员
•行为分析师/行为专家
•临床心理学家
•课堂同伴/朋友
• 兄弟姐妹
•大家庭，特别是普通护理人员
然而，核心治疗团队（负责实施实际干预的团队）包括父母，学校环境中的关键工作者以及学校以外的心理健康专业人员（例如，行为分析师，临床心理学家）。所有成员都有不同的责任：
关键工作者是学校内部的个人，负责：
1.管理和实施学校环境中的行为干预
2.向所有学校环境和尽可能多的人推广演讲
3.与老师，家长和治疗心理学家沟通
最常见的是，关键工作者是具有心理健康或语言/语言背景和培训的人，例如学校心理学家，行为分析师，社会工作者，辅导员或语言/语言病理学家。但是，确定一个能够定期与孩子见面的关键工作者很困难 - 专业人员工作量过大，案件量很大，学校可能只有有限数量的特殊教育人员。因此，关键工作者的功能或现实标准是能够始终与孩子一起工作，关心和干预的人，愿意灵活并且更多地了解选择性缄默症，并且是孩子的良好个性匹配。我一直在治疗团队，其中主要工作人员是校长，学校护士，助教，辅助专业人员和阅读专家等。不建议老师作为关键工作者。这不是因为培训，能力水平或欲望，而仅仅是因为教师通常有30多个孩子经常接受护理，并且有时间定期与特定孩子一对一关注，可能会对已经有压力的孩子施加过多的压力工作。通常最好是另一个人取得关键工作者的职位，但与老师密切沟通。
关键工作者的另一个重要特征是可持续干预的可用性。今天的学校鼓励团体退出特殊教育服务。这可能对选择性缄默症的孩子不起作用 - 至少在开始时是这样。虽然有一些值得注意的例外（例如孩子在与朋友或兄弟姐妹在一起时更容易说话），为了让大多数孩子获得成功，他们需要先与一对一的主要工作人员见面经常互动。为了取得持续和重大的进展，每周三次与孩子单独会面15分钟是最低限度的必要条件。这有一个很好的理由，这些孩子每天都在练习避免策略，每天多次。为了改变他们的行为曲目，我们需要每天多次练习勇敢的行为。实践越频繁越好。如果每周练习次数少于三次，则很难获得牵引力并取得进展。每周一次或每隔一周开会一次，这是学校大多数特殊教育服务的标准，根本不足以鼓励变革。
一些学校通过命名两名关键工作者来处理这个问题。例如，社会工作者可能每周与孩子会面两次，并且SLP可能每周与他会面两次。在学校内建立多个沟通合作伙伴是很棒的，如果两个核心工作者经常互相沟通，这通常很有效。应该预期孩子一开始可能会稍微慢一点，因为他们必须对两个不同的人而不是一个人脱敏。
学校以外的心理健康专业人员负责：
1.通过与学校协商制定和维护治疗计划。
2.培训家长采取策略，加强与大家庭/朋友和公共场所的沟通。
3.在公共场合进行“勇敢的练习”，例如在餐厅点餐，打电话给人，询问在商店里找物品的地方，以及在操场上接近同龄人。
在寻找心理学家，行为分析师或心理健康专业人士协助治疗时，寻找一些关键特征非常重要：
•与儿童，特别是患有焦虑症的儿童一起工作的经验。
•在行为治疗或认知行为治疗方面的培训和经验，以及将这些作为主要治疗方式的用途。
•愿意学习选择性缄默症（因为它与其他儿童焦虑症略有不同）。
•与家长，孩子和学校保持良好的关系，并愿意与这三者充分合作。
如果您的精神卫生专业人员在您所在地区治疗选择性缄默症方面具有专业知识或丰富经验。 。 。你很幸运。这些人可以在Selective Mutism Group网站www.selectivemutism.org上找到（在“查找帮助”下搜索）。大多数地区的后院都没有专家，因此父母必须通过良好的，老式的侦探工作找到具有上述资格的人（也称为打电话给心理学家的办公室并提出正确的问题）。如果在经济上可行的话，鼓励父母就专业知识，能力和健康状况做出关于治疗专业人员的决定 - 而不是他们是否接受孩子的健康保险。虽然可以理解的是，财务方面的考虑对于家庭来说是重要和必要的，但有效的早期干预是必不可少的，在无效的治疗中浪费时间可能不值得省钱。

Parental responsibilities include:
1. Advocating for the child in the school setting. 
2. Maintaining documentation of everything (school meetings, recommendations by the psychologist, teacher notes, progress notes, etc.). 
3. Communicating with the teacher, keyworker, and clinical psychologist about progress. 
4. Practicing regularly in public and with extended family and friends; this often means creating opportunities for the child to speak (this will be discussed later in Chapter Five).
父母责任包括：
1.在学校环境中倡导孩子。
2.维护所有内容的文件（学校会议，心理学家的建议，教师笔记，进度记录等）。
3.与老师，关键工作者和临床心理学家沟通进展情况。
4.定期在公共场合和大家庭和朋友一起练习; 这通常意味着为孩子创造机会（这将在后面的第五章中讨论）。

BUILD RAPPORT
Once a treatment team is in place and the keyworker(s) have been determined, adults on the treatment team are encouraged to focus on building a positive rapport with the child. In general treatment outcome studies, the best predictor of treatment effectiveness is the relationship between patient and therapist.
Selective mutism is no exception. This section on building rapport is mainly targeted to the treating professional or school personnel (e.g., teacher, keyworker, etc.), and the strategies outlined below are highly recommended for the first few sessions between the adult and a child with selective mutism. However, parents can also support the child’s social and emotional growth by spending concentrated one-on-one time with the child with positive feedback for engaging in verbal interactions. Concentrated one-on-one time, or “special time” as I call it, is based on skills taught in parent child interaction therapy (PCIT), a therapeutic intervention developed to enhance the adult-child relationship by changing the interaction between the two parties. Although it is commonly used for young children with conduct disorder issues, it can also be very beneficial with children with selective mutism to provide positive and rewarding interactions with parents, allow for structured warm-up time with new individuals, and build rapport with novel adults (McNeil, 2010).
The “Do’s” for rapport building or “special time” include:
• Play in a one-on-one setting. It is almost impossible to completely focus on a child and make them
feel comfortable if there are other children, siblings, and adults entering and exiting the interaction.
• Play at the child’s level and follow their lead. Activities that lend themselves well to this time include open-ended, creative play such as arts and crafts, constructional toys such as Legos, building blocks, and magnetic blocks, dollhouses with miniature people, and marble run sets. With older children, developmentally-appropriate (i.e., more complex) arts and crafts projects, board games, apps, etc. can be used to interact.
• Turn off all cell phones and distractions.
• PRIDE skills—rapport-building techniques that enhance the relationship.
PRIDE Skills


*Adapted from McNeil, 2010.

建立关系
一旦治疗团队到位并且确定了关键工作者，就鼓励治疗团队的成年人专注于与孩子建立积极的关系。在一般治疗结果研究中，治疗有效性的最佳预测因素是患者与治疗师之间的关系。
选择性缄默也不例外。关于建立关系的这一部分主要针对治疗专业人员或学校人员（例如，教师，关键工作者等），并且强烈建议在成人和选择性缄默儿童之间的前几个阶段中概述下面概述的策略。然而，父母也可以通过与孩子一对一地集中进行口头互动来积极反馈，从而支持孩子的社交和情感发展。我称之为一对一时间或“特殊时间”，是基于父母儿童互动疗法（PCIT）所教授的技能，这是一种治疗干预措施，旨在通过改变两者之间的相互作用来增强成人与儿童的关系。派对。虽然它通常用于有行为障碍问题的幼儿，但对于有选择性缄默症的儿童来说，与父母提供积极和有益的互动，允许与新个体进行有组织的预热时间，并与新成人建立融洽关系，这也是非常有益的。 （麦克尼尔，2010年）。
建立关系或“特殊时间”的“做”包括：
•以一对一的方式进行游戏。几乎不可能完全专注于孩子并制造它们
如果有其他孩子，兄弟姐妹和成年人进入和退出互动，我会感到很舒服。
•在孩子的水平上玩并跟随他们的领先。此时非常适合的活动包括开放式的创意游戏，如艺术和手工艺品，建筑玩具，如乐高积木，积木，磁性积木，带微型人的玩具屋和大理石跑步套装。对于年龄较大的儿童，可以使用适合发展的（即更复杂的）艺术和手工艺项目，棋盘游戏，应用程序等进行交互。
•关闭所有手机和分心。
•PRIDE技能 - 建立融洽关系的技巧。
PRIDE技能





Many individuals are confused by the difference between reflections and behavioral descriptions. Reflections are a restatement of anything the child communicates, either verbally or nonverbally. For example, if the child made a verbal comment, a reflection would simply restate that comment (taking care not to turn the reflection into a question by adding an inflection to your voice). If the child nodded or pointed, a reflection would acknowledge that nonverbal communication. A behavioral description is much like the job of a sports announcer—it is providing the commentary of what is occurring in the play interaction.
“Special time” can be used to build rapport with a novel (or less familiar) adult. My first session(s) almost always include PRIDE skills, as the children are usually nervous and apprehensive when first meeting me, and my first goal in treatment is to build rapport. If there is one thing we know about children, it is that if they like you and respect you (and feel respected by you), they will try so very hard to accomplish the goals you set. If they don’t like you or don’t feel a rapport with you, they will likely do. . . nothing. PRIDE skills can be used by teachers when they first meet a new student, keyworkers when they begin working with the student in the school setting, clinical psychologists at the first session or few sessions to build rapport, parents to enhance the relationship and calm their child when the situation is overwhelming. The list of possible opportunities for use goes on and on.
The goal of “special time” with keyworkers or novel adults is to build rapport, but parents can also utilize “special time” to develop and practice interpersonal and social skills. This “special time” with parents can be particularly beneficial if paired with role-playing of typical, real-life communicative situations. Since speaking can be a high-stress and multi-faceted skill, scripting (e.g., developing an over-practiced language routine) can reduce anxiety and increase the likelihood that speech will just “slip out.” For example, most children regularly go out to a restaurant to order, the café to buy a donut, or the store to purchase an item. For young children, role-playing of these activities can be done within pretend play situations. Some examples of role-playing could include:
许多人对反思和行为描述之间的差异感到困惑。反思是对孩子所传达的任何事物的重述，无论是口头还是非口头。例如，如果孩子发表了口头评论，那么反思就会重新发表评论（注意不要通过在你的声音中添加一个变形来将反思变成一个问题）。如果孩子点头或指出，反思会承认非语言交流。行为描述很像体育播音员的工作 - 它提供了对游戏互动中发生的事情的评论。
“特殊时间”可用于与新颖（或不太熟悉）的成年人建立融洽关系。我的第一次会议几乎总是包括PRIDE技能，因为孩子们在第一次见到我时通常会感到紧张和忧虑，而我的第一个治疗目标是建立融洽关系。如果我们对孩子有一点了解，那就是如果他们喜欢你并尊重你（并且感到受到你的尊重），他们会非常努力地完成你设定的目标。如果他们不喜欢你或者与你没有关系，他们很可能会这样做。 。 。没有。教师在初次见到新学生时可以使用PRIDE技能，当他们开始在学校环境中与学生一起工作时，可以使用PRIDE技能，在第一次会议上使用临床心理学家或在几次会议中建立融洽关系，父母可以加强关系并让孩子平静下来当情况势不可挡时。可能的使用机会清单一直在继续。
与关键工作者或小说成年人“特殊时间”的目标是建立融洽关系，但父母也可以利用“特殊时间”来发展和实践人际关系和社交技能。如果与典型的，真实的交际情境的角色扮演相结合，与父母的这个“特殊时间”可能特别有益。由于说话可能是一种高压力和多方面的技能，脚本（例如，开发过度练习的语言例程）可以减少焦虑并增加言语“滑出”的可能性。例如，大多数孩子经常外出到餐馆订购，咖啡馆买甜甜圈，或商店购买物品。对于幼儿来说，这些活动的角色扮演可以在假装游戏情境中完成。角色扮演的一些例子可能包括：
• Parent plays the role of storekeeper and the child “purchases” items in the store. A script
could include the child bringing the items to the counter and asking, “How much do I owe you?” After the parent responds with a price, the child hands over the money, which is good practice of handover-takeover (e.g., handing items to someone or taking items handed to them by others), says “Thank you,” and takes the bag.
• Parent plays the role of the barista at a café. The child approaches the counter and the parent
asks, “Hi, what can I get for you?” The child replies, “A donut and small apple juice” and hands over money. After handing back the change, the parent says, “Thank you.” and the child replies back, “Thanks.”
• Parent plays the role of teacher in the classroom, and asks the child an academic question. The
child raises their hand and waits to be called on. When the “teacher” calls on them, the child responds verbally, and the parent praises the brave talking.
Of course, it is also great to switch roles so that the child has the opportunity to play the role of storekeeper, barista, and teacher. The key to this pretend play is over-practice; the child already has a concept of what to say in these situations (this is not new to them), but in order for the speech to come easily it must be over-practiced so that it is simply a natural response to the interaction. Additionally, repeated language routines in real life frequently help the child build confidence and become verbal. For example, a family I worked with went to the same Barnes and Noble® each week to order a cookie. It became part of their routine, and, with practice, their daughter was eventually able to order a cookie independently.
•父扮演店主的角色，孩子在商店中“购买”物品。一个脚本
可能包括孩子将物品带到柜台并询问“我欠你多少钱？”在父母以价格回应之后，孩子交出了钱，这是交接接管的好习惯（例如，处理物品）对某人或他人交给他们的物品，“谢谢你”，并拿走了包。
•父母在咖啡馆扮演咖啡师的角色。孩子接近柜台和父母
问：“嗨，我能得到什么？”孩子回答说，“一个甜甜圈和小苹果汁”并交出钱。在交回更改后，父母说：“谢谢你。”孩子回答说，“谢谢。”
•家长在课堂上扮演教师的角色，并向孩子询问学业问题。该
孩子举起手，等待被召唤。当“老师”打电话给他们时，孩子会口头回应，而父母称赞勇敢的说话。
当然，转换角色也很棒，这样孩子就有机会扮演店主，咖啡师和老师的角色。这种假装游戏的关键是过度练习;孩子已经有了在这些情境下说些什么的概念（这对他们来说并不陌生），但为了使言语变得容易，必须过度练习，这样才能简单地对交互作出自然的反应。此外，现实生活中重复的语言程序经常帮助孩子建立信心并变得口头。例如，我与之合作的一个家庭每周去同一家Barnes和Noble®订购一个饼干。它成为他们常规的一部分，并且通过练习，他们的女儿最终能够独立订购饼干。
EDUCATING THE CHILD ABOUT ANXIETY
After a positive rapport has been developed with the child (this can be in the first session or may take several sessions) the next step is educating the child about selective mutism (although it is not necessary to use the specific term) and providing them with ways to communicate about anxiety. The description and psycho-education on anxiety may look very diverse depending on the age and understanding of the child. For individuals of all ages it is helpful to explain anxiety, discuss worries about speaking, and provide a scale for communication about “how hard” certain activities may be.
The steps in psycho-education include:
1. Explain anxiety. 
2. Discuss how treatment will proceed. 
3. Provide an anxiety rating scale.
For younger children:
I don’t know if your mom or dad told you, but I work with a lot of kids who have a hard time getting their words out or talking to new people. Some kids say talking is hard because they are worried about how people might react when they talk, or maybe from feeling worried that they might not say the words right or people might not understand. Worries like that can make our stomach or head hurt, or it may make the words get stuck in our throat even when we want to talk. When that happens, it is good to start working with someone like me, because I teach kids how to feel braver and stronger. We do activities to build brave muscles.
I don’t know if you have ever seen someone with REALLY big muscles, like this (show picture of weightlifter) but these people are not just born with big muscles. Instead, they started off with almost no muscles at all. They wanted to get stronger, and so they lifted weights almost every day. They could not start out with huge weights, because they were not strong enough yet, but they lifted small weights every day until they built little muscles and got a little stronger. Then, they were able to lift medium-sized weights. At first, moving up to mediumsized weights was hard. . . and they had to push, and push, and push to get the weight up. But as they practiced every day, it got easier to lift the medium-sized weights, because their muscles were growing. Then, they were able to lift big weights.
We are going to build our brave muscles in here in the same way. First, we start with brave practices that are easy, like nodding or shaking your head to answer my question. I can promise I will never make you do anything you cannot do—in fact, you get to be in charge of our brave muscle building. We will only move up to the next “weight” when you feel like you are strong enough. Your job is to work your hardest to get stronger and braver, and from what your mom and dad tell me, you are a really hard worker. The good news is that when you practice your brave work, we will have some cool prizes. Soon, the things that seemed a little hard will get easier and easier, because you will be getting stronger and stronger.
For older children/adolescents:
I met with your mom and dad recently, and they were telling me that they notice it is hard for you to talk to people sometimes in school and in public. I don’t know if you realize this, but there are a lot of kids who have difficulty talking to people in public—in fact, when you ask kids and adults what their biggest fear is, the vast majority say speaking in public. Some kids and teenagers say that it is hard to talk because they are concerned about how others might react when they talk, or they are afraid to say something wrong or have people look at them funny. There is a name for this anxiety about talking to people in school or public; it is called selective mutism.
Sometimes anxiety is a good thing. If we did not have anxiety, people might not be careful when they drive, or not study for big tests, or might not be prepared for work. A little anxiety helps keep us safe and motivated. However, sometimes our brains give us too big of a “warning signal” when we are anxious. I am not sure if you have ever been in a school when a fire alarm was pulled as a prank, but I have. Our class filed out the door and into the parking lot, because the siren was alerting us to go and we did not know that there wasn’t really a fire. Sometimes our brains send us the alarm signal when it is not really necessary. We call these “brain tricks.” The good news is, we can start controlling these brain tricks and get stronger and braver through slow practice of things that are hard.
Let me give you an example. If I were learning how to ride a bike, I would not just jump on a regular bike and ride down a huge hill. That would be way too scary. Instead, I would start with riding on a flat sidewalk with training wheels, and when that was easy I might take the training wheels off and have someone hold the back of the bike steady while I ride. When that is easy, I could ask them to let go but still ride on a flat sidewalk until I was good. Then, I would feel comfortable to ride on increasingly bigger hills, because I have practiced. The thing that seemed too hard at first (riding a regular bike down a big hill) now seems easy because I practiced the little steps slowly.
That is what I am hoping you and I can do together. My job is to help you with the slow steps —to be your coach. I will never ask you to do anything you cannot do—in fact, you get to be in charge of letting me know when you are ready to move to the next step. Your job is to try as hard as you can to be brave even when it is hard, because if you just stick in there and practice, it quickly gets easier and easier, until it is not hard at all anymore.
It is important to remember that education on anxiety is not something you do one single time. These statements will need to be repeated and revisited with children several times. The more kids understand why they are practicing and what the eventual goal is (for them to feel braver and stronger), the more internally motivated they are. At times, it is helpful to use a visual representation similar to the conceptualization of selective mutism. I use an “avoidance cycle” and “brave cycle” to teach older children about the reasoning behind brave practices.

教育孩子关于焦虑
在与孩子建立了积极的关系（这可能是在第一次会议或可能需要几次会议）之后，下一步是教育孩子选择性缄默症（虽然没有必要使用特定术语）并为他们提供关于焦虑的沟通方式。焦虑的描述和心理教育可能看起来非常多样化，取决于孩子的年龄和理解。对于所有年龄段的个人来说，解释焦虑，讨论对口语的担忧以及提供关于某些活动可能“有多难”的沟通的量表是有帮助的。
心理教育的步骤包括：
1.解释焦虑。
2.讨论如何进行治疗。
3.提供焦虑评定量表。
对于年幼的孩子：
我不知道你的妈妈或爸爸是否告诉过你，但是我和很多孩子一起工作，他们很难说出来或者和新人交谈。有些孩子说说话很难，因为他们担心人们在谈话时会有什么反应，或者担心他们可能不会说正确的话或人们可能不理解。这样的担心可能会使我们的胃或头部受伤，或者即使我们想说话，也可能会使话语卡在我们的喉咙里。当发生这种情况时，开始与像我这样的人一起工作是很好的，因为我教孩子们如何更勇敢和更强壮。我们开展活动来锻炼勇敢的肌肉。
我不知道你是否见过像这样的大肌肉的人（展示举重运动员的照片），但这些人不仅仅是天生就有大块肌肉。相反，他们开始几乎没有任何肌肉。他们想要变得更强壮，所以他们几乎每天都举重。他们不能从巨大的重量开始，因为他们还不够强壮，但他们每天都举起小重量，直到他们建立一点肌肉并且变得更强壮。然后，他们能够提升中等重量。起初，升级到中等重量很难。 。 。他们不得不推动，推动和推动以增加体重。但是当他们每天练习时，因为他们的肌肉在增长，所以更容易抬起中等重量的体重。然后，他们能够举起大重量。
我们将以同样的方式在这里建立我们勇敢的肌肉。首先，我们从容易开始的勇敢实践开始，比如点头或摇头回答我的问题。我可以保证，我永远不会让你做任何你不能做的事情 - 事实上，你必须负责我们勇敢的肌肉建设。当你觉得自己足够强壮时，我们只能提升到下一个“体重”。你的工作就是努力工作，变得更强壮，更勇敢，从你妈妈和爸爸告诉我的，你是一个非常努力的工作者。好消息是，当你练习勇敢的工作时，我们会有一些很酷的奖品。很快，看起来有点困难的事情将变得更容易和更容易，因为你会越来越强大。
适合年龄较大的儿童/青少年：
我最近遇到了你的妈妈和爸爸，他们告诉我他们注意到你很难在学校和公共场合与人交谈。我不知道你是否意识到这一点，但是有很多孩子在公共场合与人交谈很困难 - 事实上，当你向孩子和成年人询问他们最大的恐惧是什么时，绝大多数人都说在公共场合说话。有些孩子和青少年说他们很难说话，因为他们担心别人在谈话时会有什么反应，或者他们害怕说错话或让人们看起来很有趣。关于与学校或公众人士交谈的焦虑有一个名字;它被称为选择性缄默症。
有时候焦虑是一件好事。如果我们没有焦虑，人们在开车时可能不会小心，或者不进行大型测试，或者可能没有为工作做好准备。有点焦虑有助于保持我们的安全和积极性。然而，当我们焦虑时，有时候我们的大脑给了我们太大的“警告信号”。我不确定你是否曾经在学校里当火灾报警器作为恶作剧被拉，但我有。我们的班级出门进了停车场，因为警笛警告我们走了，我们不知道没有真正的火灾。有时我们的大脑会在没有必要时向我们发送警报信号。我们将这些称为“大脑技巧”。好消息是，我们可以开始控制这些大脑技巧，并通过缓慢练习那些艰难的事情来变得更加强大和勇敢。
让我举一个例子。如果我正在学习如何骑自行车，我不会只是跳上普通的自行车而是骑在一个巨大的山坡上。那太可怕了。相反，我会开始骑在带有训练轮的平坦人行道上，当这很容易时，我可能会取下训练轮，并且当我骑车时，有人会保持自行车的后部稳定。如果这很容易，我可以让他们放手，但仍然骑在平坦的人行道上，直到我好。然后，我会觉得骑在越来越大的山上会很舒服，因为我练习过。一开始看起来太难的事情（在一个大山上骑一辆普通的自行车）现在看起来很容易，因为我慢慢地练习了一些小步骤。
这就是我希望你和我能在一起做的事情。我的工作是帮助你采取缓慢的步骤 - 成为你的教练。我绝不会要求你做任何你不能做的事情 - 事实上，当你准备好进入下一步时，你需要负责让我知道。你的工作就是尽可能努力地勇敢，即使在艰难时也要勇敢，因为如果你只是坚持在那里练习，它会很快变得更容易，更容易，直到它不再那么难。
重要的是要记住，焦虑教育不是你一次性做的事情。这些陈述需要多次重复并与孩子一起重访。越多的孩子理解他们为什么练习以及最终的目标是什么（让他们感到更勇敢和更强壮），他们的内心动机就越多。有时，使用类似于选择性缄默症概念化的视觉表示是有帮助的。我使用“回避周期”和“勇敢的周期”来教育年龄较大的孩子勇敢实践背后的原因。

The Avoidance Cycle 逃避循环
Next, kids are taught to assess and report their own anxiety level. A five-point scale provides enough information without being overwhelming to children. For younger children and visual learners, I use a visual 1-5 scale. This may take the form of a mercury thermometer or a progression of five faces (progressing from happy to sad). The Incredible 5-Point Scale is a wonderful resource for assisting kids in visually understanding how to rate their anxiety (Dunn Buron, 2007). I generally do not ask children to rate how “scary” or “anxiety-provoking” an activity is, as many children with selective mutism do not describe feelings of “fear” or “anxiety.” Instead, children are asked how “hard” they think it will be or how “hard” it really was. Ratings occur at four different time points in treatment:
• At the start of treatment, to help build a step-wise ladder (communication ladder) of communication activities. It is helpful to know what the child perceives as especially hard, since my estimation of the difficulty level of the activity and their perception may not be the same.
• Prior to “brave practices”—“Okay, we get to practice answering a short question from Ms. Mensel.
How hard do you think this will be?”
• After a “brave practice” has been completed—“That was great. Now tell me how hard it really was
to answer that question from Ms. Mensel.”

接下来，教孩子们评估和报告他们自己的焦虑水平。五分制可以提供足够的信息，而不会给孩子们带来压力。对于年幼的孩子和视觉学习者，我使用视觉1-5级。这可能采取水银温度计的形式或五个面孔的进展（从快乐到悲伤）。令人难以置信的5点量表是一个很好的资源，可以帮助孩子在视觉上理解如何评价他们的焦虑（Dunn Buron，2007）。我一般不会要求孩子评价一种活动是如何“吓人”或“引起焦虑”，因为许多有选择性缄默症的孩子并没有描述“恐惧”或“焦虑”的感觉。相反，孩子们会被问到“难”。他们认为这将是真正的或者有多么“难”。评分发生在治疗的四个不同时间点：
•在治疗开始时，帮助建立沟通活动的阶梯式阶梯（沟通阶梯）。知道孩子认为特别难的是有帮助的，因为我对活动的难度和他们的感知的估计可能不一样。
•在“勇敢的做法”之前 - “好的，我们练习回答Mensel女士的一个简短问题。
你认为这会有多难？“
•在“勇敢的练习”完成之后 - “那很棒。现在告诉我它真的有多难
从Mensel女士那里回答这个问题。“
The Brave Cycle

• As a summary of progression in treatment, such as a reminder of how far the child has come—“Do you remember that answering me used to be a 4, but now you have told me that it is down to a 1. You have gotten so much braver and stronger. You are really building those brave muscles.”
Asking a child to report how hard they believe an activity will be and how hard it really was provides an opportunity for feedback on the “brain tricks” mentioned earlier. A key characteristic of anxiety disorders is an over-estimation of how bad the feared activity will be. Ninety percent of the time, children will respond that the actual practice was much easier than they had expected it to be, and it allows the adult to respond, “Wow—that practice ended up being easier than you thought it would be. We should remember this—our brains sometimes trick us in to believing that our brave practices will be harder than they really end up being. Next time you think a practice will be hard, can I remind you of this experience and how you are a lot braver than you think?”
Of course, all kids have the occasional experience of a practice that is just as hard as, or even harder, than they had hypothesized. When that occurs, adults can again provide helpful, constructive feedback including:
“Okay, it is good for us to know that answering Ms. Mensel was as hard as you thought it was going to be. I am so impressed that even though you thought it was going to be a 3, and you were right in your rating, you were still able to do it. That just shows what a brave kid you are, that you will try something even though it is kind of hard. That is the kind of practice that makes us so much braver and stronger.”
Or: “So you had thought it would be a 3, but when you really went up to answer Ms. Mensel, it jumped up to a 5. Yikes. Let’s think of a way that you could still practice being brave, but maybe something a little easier than answering with two words. Maybe we could have you answer with one short word or just a sound, or maybe we could have Ms. Mensel turn away from you as you answer. What do you think?”
•作为治疗进展的总结，例如提醒孩子已经到了多远 - “你还记得回答过去曾经是4岁，但是现在你告诉我它已经下降到了1岁。你有得到了这么多的勇敢和坚强。你真的正在建立那些勇敢的肌肉。“
要求孩子报告他们认为某项活动有多难以及实际上有多难，这为前面提到的“大脑技巧”提供了反馈机会。焦虑症的一个关键特征是高估了恐惧活动的严重程度。百分之九十的时间，孩子会回答说实际练习比他们预期的要容易得多，并且它允许成年人做出回应，“哇 - 这种练习最终比你想象的要容易。我们应该记住这一点 - 我们的大脑有时会诱使我们相信我们的勇敢实践会比他们真正最终成为更难。下次你认为练习会很困难时，我能否提醒你这种经历以及你比你想象的更勇敢？“
当然，所有孩子偶尔会有一种与他们假设的一样艰难甚至更难的练习经历。当发生这种情况时，成年人可以再次提供有益的建设性反馈，包括：
“好的，我们知道回答Mensel女士的想法和你想象的一样难。我印象非常深刻，即使你认为它会是3，你的评分是对的，你仍然可以做到。这只是表明你是一个勇敢的孩子，你会尝试一些东西，即使它有点难。这种做法让我们变得更加勇敢和坚强。“
或者：“所以你以为它会是一个3，但是当你真的回答Mensel女士时，它会上升到5.Yikes。让我们想一想你仍然可以练习勇敢的方式，但也许比用两个单词回答要容易一些。也许我们可以让你用一个简短的单词或只是一个声音来回答，或者也许我们可以让Mensel女士在回答时离开你。你怎么看？”


CONTINGENCY MANAGEMENT
I know, I know—you have already tried reward systems, and they have not worked. Many parents report to me that they have tried reward systems in the past. They offered their child a new iPod® and a pony if the child would just talk. . . but that still did not work to increase speech. If you are reading this book, you have probably already realized that for most children with selective mutism, reward systems by themselves are useless. However, contingency management can be a very useful tool in conjunction with a good intervention plan.
Contingency management: Making verbalizations more rewarding and nonverbal responding or a lack of responding less rewarding.
We have all done it. We have all made it very easy for the child to get what they want and need, and to convey information via facial expressions, writing, nonverbal gestures, or another mode of silent communication. This allowance does help the child become comfortable and successful in the school setting. It does not, nonetheless, increase verbalizations. I recently had a session with a third grader with selective mutism. We were discussing his progression to talking to peers in school and a few specific adults, but he has yet to speak to his teacher. When I asked him what was keeping him from including his teacher in his “talking circle,” he replied, “She gives me the white board and lets me write all of my answers.” This teacher was making nonverbal responding very rewarding for him.
There are many ways of utilizing contingency management when working with kids with selective mutism. First, we need to offer them the opportunity to talk, usually through asking questions that prompt or encourage a verbal response. The best way to do this is through forced-choice (or multiple- choice) questions. When children with selective mutism are asked a question in a yes/no format, it immediately and naturalistically offers up a nonverbal response (nodding or shaking their head). When they are ask a question in an open-ended manner, it may be overwhelming—the child may be caught trying to decide how to respond, what the “correct” response is, what the asker really wants to hear, etc. Instead, if the child is provided with a few options for a response, we increase the likelihood that he or she will answer verbally. Trying to change our questions to forced-choice takes some practice. Let us try changing a few yes/no and open-ended questions to forced-choice questions.
应急管理
我知道，我知道 - 你已经尝试过奖励制度，而且他们没有工作过。许多家长向我汇报他们过去曾尝试过奖励系统。如果孩子只是说话，他们会给孩子一个新的iPod®和一匹小马。 。 。但这仍然无法增加言论。如果你正在读这本书，你可能已经意识到，对于大多数有选择性缄默的孩子来说，奖励制度本身是无用的。但是，应急管理可以成为一个非常有用的工具，与良好的干预计划相结合。
应急管理：使言语能力更有价值，非语言反应或缺乏回应而不那么有益。
我们都做到了。我们都让孩子很容易得到他们想要和需要的东西，并通过面部表情，写作，非语言手势或其他静默沟通方式传达信息。这项津贴确实有助于孩子在学校环境中获得舒适和成功。尽管如此，它并没有增加言语能力。我最近与三年级学生一起选择性缄默症。我们正在讨论他在学校和一些特定成年人中与同龄人交谈的进展，但他还没有和他的老师说话。当我问他是什么让他不让他的老师加入他的“谈话圈子”时，他回答道，“她给了我白板，让我写下我所有的答案。”这位老师正在做非语言回应对他非常有益。
在与选择性缄默症的孩子一起工作时，有很多方法可以利用应急管理。首先，我们需要为他们提供谈话的机会，通常是通过提出提示或鼓励口头答复的问题。最好的方法是通过强制选择（或多选）问题。当选择性缄默症的儿童被问及是/否格式的问题时，它立即和自然地提供非语言反应（点头或摇头）。当他们以开放式的方式提问时，可能会让人感到压力 - 孩子可能会被抓住，试图决定如何回应，“正确”的反应是什么，提问者真正想要听到的是什么，等等。如果为孩子提供了一些回答选项，我们会增加他或她口头回答的可能性。试图将我们的问题改为强制选择需要一些练习。让我们尝试将一些是/否和开放式问题改为强制选择问题。

Open-Ended Example: “What did you do this weekend?” 
Forced Choice Question: “Did you ride your bike, play videogames, or something different this weekend?” 
Yes/No Example: “Do you like dogs?” 
Forced Choice Question: “Do you like dogs or not like dogs?” 
Open-Ended Question: “What is your favorite color?” 
Forced Choice Question:
Open-Ended Question: “How is your little brother feeling?” 
Forced Choice Question:Yes/No Question: “Did you dress up this year for Halloween? 
Forced Choice Question: Yes/No Question: “Do you want to work on your math right now? 
Forced Choice Question:

开放式示例：“这个周末你做了什么？”
强迫选择问题：“这个周末你骑自行车，玩电子游戏还是其他不同的东西？”
是/否示例：“你喜欢狗吗？”
强迫选择问题：“你喜欢狗还是不喜欢狗？”
开放式问题：“你最喜欢的颜色是什么？”
强迫选择问题：
开放式问题：“你的小弟弟感觉如何？”
强迫选择问题：是/否问题：“你今年为万圣节装扮了吗？
强迫选择问题：是/否问题：“你现在想要数学吗？
强迫选择问题：

Beware—sometimes we may not provide the “right” response, and the child may not answer. If the child does not respond, you may first want to change your possible answers. For example, if you ask a child, “What do you like best—dogs or cats?” and the child does not like either dogs or cats, they may not answer you. You may need to revise the question to, “What do you like better—dogs, cats, both, or neither?”
When a child responds to a forced-choice question, that response should first be reflected. Reflection consists simply of rewording or repeating the child’s response back to him. Then, the adult verbally reinforces the response. It is always important to reinforce the child verbally and through your body language. Many children with SM do not like to receive a lot of overt praise for their brave behaviors. They fear people making a big deal out of their talking, which is one of the reasons they avoid speech. In a recent workshop, a participant described a situation in which a child with SM in her school had finally spoken to the teacher. In her excitement, the teacher stopped class immediately, brought everyone’s attention to the child and congratulated him with a huge amount of praise for talking, cancelled the rest of the class day, and threw a party. The child did not return to school for two weeks. . . and when he did return he never spoke again. Please don’t be that teacher.
Verbal reinforcement should be relatively low-key and matter-of-fact, such as saying, “Thanks for letting me know” or “Great job using your brave voice.” In order not to call attention to the child, I may just give a thumbs-up or a pat on the back, or just whisper in their ear what a great job they did.
A sample interaction consisting of forced-choice questions, reflections, and labeled praise for responding could include:
请注意 - 有时我们可能无法提供“正确”的回复，而且孩子可能无法回答。如果孩子没有回应，您可能首先想要更改您的答案。例如，如果你问一个孩子，“你最喜欢什么狗或猫？”而且孩子不喜欢狗或猫，他们可能不会回答你。你可能需要修改这个问题，“你更喜欢什么 - 狗，猫，两者，或两者都没有？”
当孩子对强制选择问题作出回应时，应首先反映该回答。反思只是简单地重复或重复儿童对他的回应。然后，成年人口头强化了反应。通过口头和肢体语言强化孩子是非常重要的。许多SM患儿不喜欢因勇敢的行为而受到很多公开表扬。他们害怕人们在说话时大肆宣传，这是他们避免言论的原因之一。在最近的一次研讨会上，一位与会者描述了一种情况，即学校里有SM的孩子终于和老师说过了。在她的兴奋中，老师立即停课，引起了所有人对孩子的注意，并祝贺他在谈话中获得了巨大的赞誉，取消了上课的其余时间，并举办了派对。孩子没有回到学校两个星期。 。 。当他回来时，他再也没有说过话。请不要当那位老师。
口头强化应该是相对低调和事实上的事实，例如说“谢谢你让我知道”或“很棒的工作使用你勇敢的声音。”为了不引起对孩子的注意，我可能只是给竖起大拇指或轻拍在他们的背上，或者只是耳边低语他们做得很棒。
由强制选择问题，反思和响应标记的赞美组成的示例交互可包括：

Mom: What would you like to do today—go to the park, play on the trampoline, or something different? 
Child: Go to the park. 
Mom: Go to the park! Thanks for letting me know. I love going to the park. Should we go to Whittier Park or the park downtown? 
Child: I want to go to the one with the big slides. 
Mom: Oh, the one with the big slides—that is a really fun one. That’s Whittier Park. Are you going to try going down the biggest slide, or the medium-sized slides? 
Child: I’m going to go down the biggest one this time. 
Mom: Thanks for telling me that you want to go on the biggest one. You are getting so brave.
妈妈：今天你想做什么 - 去公园，玩蹦床，还是别的什么？
孩子：去公园。
妈妈：去公园！谢谢你让我知道。我喜欢去公园。我们应该去惠蒂尔公园还是市中心的公园？
孩子：我想去一个有大幻灯片的人。
妈妈：哦，那个有大滑梯的人 - 这真的很有趣。那是惠蒂尔公园。你打算试试最大的幻灯片，还是中型幻灯片？
孩子：这次我要去最大的一个。
妈妈：谢谢你告诉我你要继续做最大的一个。你变得如此勇敢。

After we have increased the likelihood that the child will respond to us verbally, we must have a reinforcement system in place. The most common reinforcers or rewards are:
• Obtaining desired outcome—the child gets what they want through verbal communication as
opposed to using nonverbal communication to get all needs met.
• Verbal Reinforcement—verbal praise or body language suggesting pleasure/proudness.
• Tangible Reinforcement—prizes such as toys, special outings, or belongings.
